Figure 1.1: Thesis Objectives. This work aims to advance query-guided video understanding by addressing three core questions: where objects are present, how they interact, and when specific actions occur. Consider a football match: understandin<sub>g</sub> the <sub>p</sub>la<sub>y</sub> re<sub>q</sub>uires (i) identif<sub>y</sub>in<sub>g</sub> ke<sub>y</sub> entities such as the ball, <sub>g</sub>oal<sub>p</sub>osts, and the <sub>p</sub>la<sub>y</sub>ers (where im<sub>p</sub>ortant objects are), (ii) reco<sub>g</sub>nizin<sub>g</sub> interactions like “<sub>p</sub>la<sub>y</sub>er kickin<sub>g</sub> the ball to a teammate" (how objects interact), and (iii) localizin<sub>g</sub> ke<sub>y</sub> events such “<sub>g</sub>oa" moment (when critical actions occur). To<sub>g</sub>ether, these tasks enable structured inter<sub>p</sub>retation of com<sub>p</sub>lex, fast-<sub>p</sub>aced <sub>spo</sub>rt<sub>s</sub> <sub>sce</sub>n<sub>es,</sub> m<sub>ov</sub>in<sub>g</sub> b<sub>eyo</sub>nd fr<sub>a</sub>m<sub>e-</sub>l<sub>eve</sub>l r<sub>ecog</sub>niti<sub>o</sub>n t<sub>o</sub>w<sub>a</sub>rd t<sub>e</sub>m<sub>po</sub>r<sub>a</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> <sub>g</sub>r<sub>ou</sub>nd<sub>e</sub>d<sub>,</sub> int<sub>e</sub>r<sub>ac</sub>ti<sub>o</sub>n<sub>-a</sub>w<sub>a</sub>r<sub>e</sub> <sub>un</sub>d<sub>ers</sub>t<sub>an</sub>di<sub>ng.</sub>

To improve expressiveness, multi-head attention (MHA) extends this mechanism by projecting in<sub>pu</sub>t<sub>s</sub> int<sub>o</sub> ℎ dif<sub>e</sub>r<sub>e</sub>nt <sub>su</sub>b<sub>spaces,</sub> <sub>co</sub>m<sub>pu</sub>tin<sub>g</sub> <sub>a</sub>tt<sub>e</sub>nti<sub>o</sub>n ind<sub>epe</sub>nd<sub>e</sub>ntl<sub>y</sub> in <sub>eac</sub>h<sub>,</sub> <sub>a</sub>nd <sub>co</sub>n<sub>ca</sub>t<sub>e</sub>n<sub>a</sub>tin<sub>g</sub> th<sub>e resu</sub>lt<sub>s:</sub>

• Patch embedding: Instead of words, images are split into patches $x _ { i } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { P ^ { 2 } C }$ an<sup>d</sup> projecte<sup>d</sup> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>o</sub> �<sub>-</sub>di<sub>mens</sub>i<sub>ona</sub>l <sub>em</sub>b<sub>e</sub>ddi<sub>ngs,</sub> <sub>ana</sub>l<sub>ogous</sub> t<sub>o</sub> NLP t<sub>o</sub>k<sub>en</sub> <sub>em</sub>b<sub>e</sub>ddi<sub>ngs.</sub>

## Multimodality.

• Text: Standard subword tokenization (e.g., BPE) with embeddings plus positional encod-<sup>i</sup>n<sub>g</sub>s.

• Image (CLIP): A ViT-style vision encoder produces patch embeddings, while a Transformer t<sub>ex</sub>t <sub>enco</sub>d<sub>er</sub> h<sub>an</sub>dl<sub>es</sub> <sub>su</sub>b<sub>wor</sub>d t<sub>o</sub>k<sub>ens.</sub> B<sub>o</sub>th <sub>are</sub> <sub>a</sub>li<sub>gne</sub>d <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>a</sub> <sub>con</sub>t<sub>ras</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> l<sub>oss:</sub>

• Cross-attention (ViLBERT, LXMERT): Separate unimodal encoders produce features for im<sub>age</sub> r<sub>eg</sub>i<sub>o</sub>n<sub>s</sub> <sub>a</sub>nd <sub>wo</sub>rd<sub>s.</sub> Cr<sub>oss-a</sub>tt<sub>e</sub>nti<sub>o</sub>n l<sub>aye</sub>r<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e</sub>n <sub>e</sub>x<sub>c</sub>h<sub>a</sub>n<sub>ge</sub> inf<sub>o</sub>rm<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>o</sub>n<sub>,</sub> <sub>e.g.,</sub>

• Unified tokenization (PaLI, Flamingo): All modalities are projected into a shared token space, a<sup>ll</sup>owing a sing<sup>l</sup>e Trans<sup>f</sup>ormer to atten<sup>d</sup> joint<sup>l</sup>y across t<sup>h</sup>em. Ada<sub>p</sub>tin<sub>g</sub> Transformers to vision and multimodalit<sub>y</sub> involves three main com<sub>p</sub>onents: (i) modalit<sub>y</sub>-s<sub>p</sub>ecific tokenization (e.<sub>g</sub>., ima<sub>g</sub>e <sub>p</sub>atches, s<sub>p</sub>ectro<sub>g</sub>ram frames, video cubes), (ii) <sub>p</sub>ositional encodin<sub>g</sub> schemes to ca<sub>p</sub>ture modalit<sub>y</sub> structure (1D for text, 2D for ima<sub>g</sub>es, tem<sub>p</sub>oral encodin<sub>g</sub>s for video), and (iii) interaction mechanisms such as self-attention, cross-attention, <sub>or</sub> <sub>con</sub>t<sub>ras</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> <sub>a</sub>li<sub>gnmen</sub>t<sub>.</sub> Thi<sub>s</sub> fl<sub>ex</sub>ibilit<sub>y</sub> <sub>ma</sub>k<sub>es</sub> th<sub>e</sub> T<sub>rans</sub>f<sub>ormer</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>un</sub>if<sub>y</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>arc</sub>hit<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>ure</sub> <sub>across</sub> di<sub>verse mo</sub>d<sub>a</sub>liti<sub>es.</sub>

## 2.1.3 TRANSFORMERS FOR OBJECT DETECTION

Transformers have been ada<sub>p</sub>ted to object detection throu<sub>g</sub>h DETR (DEtection TRansformer) [10], <sub>w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h f<sub>ormu</sub>l<sub>a</sub>t<sub>es</sub> d<sub>e</sub>t<sub>ec</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>as</sub> <sub>a</sub> di<sub>rec</sub>t <sub>se</sub>t <sub>pre</sub>di<sub>c</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>pro</sub>bl<sub>em.</sub> U<sub>n</sub>lik<sub>e</sub> <sub>anc</sub>h<sub>or-</sub>b<sub>ase</sub>d d<sub>e</sub>t<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>ors</sub> $( \mathrm { e . g . }$ , Faster R-CNN [45], RetinaNet [46]), DETR uses a fixed set of learnable object <sub>q</sub>ueries, which th<sub>e</sub> T<sub>rans</sub>f<sub>ormer</sub> d<sub>eco</sub>d<sub>er maps</sub> t<sub>o</sub> b<sub>oun</sub>di<sub>ng</sub> b<sub>ox coor</sub>di<sub>na</sub>t<sub>es an</sub>d <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub> l<sub>a</sub>b<sub>e</sub>l<sub>s.</sub> A CNN b<sub>ac</sub>kb<sub>one</sub> <sub>ex</sub>t<sub>rac</sub>t<sub>s</sub> i<sub>mage</sub> f<sub>ea</sub>t<sub>ures, w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h <sub>are</sub> fl<sub>a</sub>tt<sub>ene</sub>d <sub>an</sub>d f<sub>e</sub>d i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>o</sub> th<sub>e</sub> T<sub>rans</sub>f<sub>ormer enco</sub>d<sub>er.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> d<sub>eco</sub>d<sub>er</sub> cross-atten<sup>d</sup>s <sup>b</sup>etween o<sup>b</sup>ject queries an<sup>d</sup> enco<sup>d</sup>er <sup>f</sup>eatures, re<sup>fi</sup>ning pre<sup>d</sup>ictions iterative<sup>l</sup>y.

Th<sub>e</sub> l<sub>oss</sub> f<sub>unc</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>com</sub>bi<sub>nes</sub> <sub>a</sub> bi<sub>par</sub>tit<sub>e</sub> <sub>ma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>hi<sub>ng</sub> <sub>cos</sub>t <sub>an</sub>d <sub>a</sub> <sub>se</sub>t<sub>-</sub>b<sub>ase</sub>d <sub>pre</sub>di<sub>c</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> l<sub>oss:</sub>

$$
\mathcal { L } = \sum _ { ( y , \hat { y } ) \in \pi ^ { * } } \mathcal { L } _ { c l s } ( y , \hat { y } ) + \lambda \mathcal { L } _ { b o x } ( y , \hat { y } ) ,
$$

<sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> $\pi ^ { * }$ d<sub>eno</sub>t<sub>es</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>op</sub>ti<sub>ma</sub>l <sub>one-</sub>t<sub>o-one</sub> <sub>ass</sub>i<sub>gnmen</sub>t b<sub>e</sub>t<sub>ween</sub> <sub>pre</sub>di<sub>c</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>ou</sub>t<sub>pu</sub>t<sub>s</sub> <sub>�</sub>ˆ <sub>an</sub>d <sub>g</sub>r<sub>ou</sub>nd<sub>-</sub>tr<sub>u</sub>th t<sub>a</sub>r<sub>ge</sub>t<sub>s</sub> <sub>�.</sub> Thi<sub>s</sub> <sub>ass</sub>i<sub>g</sub>nm<sub>e</sub>nt i<sub>s</sub> <sub>co</sub>m<sub>pu</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>us</sub>in<sub>g</sub> th<sub>e</sub> H<sub>u</sub>n<sub>ga</sub>ri<sub>a</sub>n <sub>a</sub>l<sub>go</sub>rithm<sub>,</sub> <sub>w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h minimizes a matchin<sub>g</sub> cost. Unlike traditional detectors that rel<sub>y</sub> on heuristic <sub>p</sub>ost-<sub>p</sub>rocessin<sub>g</sub> (e.<sub>g</sub>., non-maximum suppression), this formulation treats object detection as a direct set prediction <sub>p</sub>r<sub>o</sub>bl<sub>e</sub>m<sub>,</sub> <sub>e</sub>n<sub>su</sub>rin<sub>g</sub> <sub>pe</sub>rm<sub>u</sub>t<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>o</sub>n<sub>-</sub>in<sub>va</sub>ri<sub>a</sub>nt <sub>supe</sub>r<sub>v</sub>i<sub>s</sub>i<sub>o</sub>n <sub>a</sub>nd <sub>e</sub>limin<sub>a</sub>tin<sub>g</sub> d<sub>up</sub>li<sub>ca</sub>t<sub>e</sub> <sub>p</sub>r<sub>e</sub>di<sub>c</sub>ti<sub>o</sub>n<sub>s.</sub>

DETR eliminates the need for non-maximum su<sub>pp</sub>ression (NMS) and anchor desi<sub>g</sub>n, but re<sub>q</sub>uires lon<sub>g</sub>er trainin<sub>g</sub> to conver<sub>g</sub>e. Deformable DETR [47] im<sub>p</sub>roves eficienc<sub>y</sub> b<sub>y</sub> restrictin<sub>g</sub> attention to s<sub>p</sub>arse s<sub>p</sub>atial locations usin<sub>g</sub> learned ofsets. Variants such as DAB-DETR [48] and DN-DETR [49] further accelerate conver<sub>g</sub>ence and im<sub>p</sub>rove localization.

## 2.1.4 TRANSFORMERS FOR OPEN-WORLD AND OPEN-VOCABULARY DETECTION

Transformer-Based Approaches in OVOD. In parallel, OVOD methods focus on leveraging <sub>v</sub>i<sub>s</sub>i<sub>o</sub>n<sub>-</sub>l<sub>a</sub>n<sub>guage</sub> <sub>p</sub>r<sub>e</sub>tr<sub>a</sub>inin<sub>g</sub> <sub>a</sub>nd <sub>se</sub>m<sub>a</sub>nti<sub>c</sub> <sub>supe</sub>r<sub>v</sub>i<sub>s</sub>i<sub>o</sub>n t<sub>o</sub> <sub>e</sub>n<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> <sub>voca</sub>b<sub>u</sub>l<sub>a</sub>r<sub>y</sub> <sub>e</sub>x<sub>pa</sub>n<sub>s</sub>i<sub>o</sub>n <sub>w</sub>ith<sub>ou</sub>t <sub>e</sub>x<sub>p</sub>li<sub>c</sub>it b<sub>ou</sub>ndin<sub>g-</sub>b<sub>o</sub>x <sub>a</sub>nn<sub>o</sub>t<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>o</sub>n<sub>s:</sub>

• Knowledge distillation: ViLD [63] distills knowledge from CLIP into detection heads via dual-branch learnin<sub>g</sub>. HierKD [64] and LP-OVOD [65] extend this with loss refinements <sub>an</sub>d <sub>pseu</sub>d<sub>o-</sub>l<sub>a</sub>b<sub>e</sub>li<sub>ng</sub> <sub>sc</sub>h<sub>emes</sub> t<sub>o</sub> i<sub>mprove</sub> t<sub>rans</sub>f<sub>era</sub>bilit<sub>y.</sub>

• Prompt modeling: PromptDet [69] and DetPro [70] design structured or continuous prompts to inject semantic priors. Context gra<sup>d</sup>ing an<sup>d</sup> <sup>b</sup>ac<sup>k</sup>groun<sup>d</sup>-aware prompts <sup>f</sup>urt<sup>h</sup>er <sub>re</sub>fi<sub>ne</sub> <sub>a</sub>li<sub>gnmen</sub>t <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>nove</sub>l <sub>ca</sub>t<sub>egor</sub>i<sub>es.</sub>

Ke<sub>y</sub> challen<sub>g</sub>es for video Transformers include: (1) handlin<sub>g</sub> lon<sub>g</sub> tem<sub>p</sub>oral data, (2) hi<sub>g</sub>h memor<sub>y</sub>/com<sub>p</sub>utation cost, (3) scarcit<sub>y</sub> of labeled video data, and (4) robustness to variable frame rates and motion. Common im<sub>p</sub>lementation strate<sub>g</sub>ies involve usin<sub>g</sub> CLIP [50], 2D CNNs (e.<sub>g</sub>., ResNet [74], Swin [41]) for s<sub>p</sub>atial encodin<sub>g</sub>, followed b<sub>y</sub> a tem<sub>p</sub>oral Transformer for se<sub>q</sub>uence <sub>mo</sub>d<sub>e</sub>li<sub>ng.</sub> E<sub>n</sub>d<sub>-</sub>t<sub>o-en</sub>d <sub>v</sub>id<sub>eo</sub> T<sub>rans</sub>f<sub>ormers</sub> <sub>are</sub> i<sub>ncreas</sub>i<sub>ng</sub>l<sub>y</sub> f<sub>eas</sub>ibl<sub>e</sub> <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>a</sub>d<sub>vances</sub> i<sub>n</sub> h<sub>ar</sub>d<sub>ware</sub> and eficient attention mechanisms (e.<sub>g</sub>., s<sub>p</sub>arse, linear, or axial attention).

Siamese networks [77] <sub>p</sub>ioneered <sub>p</sub>airwise similarit<sub>y</sub> learnin<sub>g</sub>, while Matchin<sub>g</sub> Networks [76] used attention over su<sub>pp</sub>ort exam<sub>p</sub>les. More recent methods like Meta-Baseline [79] inte<sub>g</sub>rate metric learnin<sub>g</sub> with <sub>p</sub>retrained b<sub>ac</sub>kb<sub>ones.</sub> Th<sub>ese</sub> <sub>me</sub>th<sub>o</sub>d<sub>s</sub> <sub>are</sub> <sub>compu</sub>t<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ona</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> <sub>e</sub>fi<sub>c</sub>i<sub>en</sub>t <sub>an</sub>d i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erpre</sub>t<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> b<sub>u</sub>t d<sub>egra</sub>d<sub>e</sub> <sub>un</sub>d<sub>er</sub> d<sub>o</sub>m<sub>a</sub>in <sub>s</sub>hift <sub>w</sub>h<sub>e</sub>n <sub>suppo</sub>rt <sub>a</sub>nd <sub>que</sub>r<sub>y</sub> di<sub>s</sub>trib<sub>u</sub>ti<sub>o</sub>n<sub>s</sub> dif<sub>e</sub>r<sub>.</sub>

(ii) Optimization-based approaches. In this family, few-shot learning is framed as metalearning across a distribution of tasks $\mathcal { T } \sim p ( \mathcal { T } )$ . Each task � is split into a support set � (used for adaptation) and a query set $\boldsymbol { Q }$ (used for evaluation). The ke<sub>y</sub> idea is to learn model parameters � th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>can</sub> b<sub>e</sub> <sub>qu</sub>i<sub>c</sub>kl<sub>y</sub> <sub>a</sub>d<sub>ap</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d t<sub>o</sub> <sub>a</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k<sub>-spec</sub>ifi<sub>c</sub> <sub>parame</sub>t<sub>er</sub> <sub>se</sub>t $\theta ^ { \prime }$ <sub>us</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>on</sub>l<sub>y</sub> �<sub>,</sub> <sub>w</sub>hil<sub>e</sub> <sub>s</sub>till <sub>ac</sub>hi<sub>ev</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>goo</sub>d <sub>ge</sub>n<sub>e</sub>r<sub>a</sub>li<sub>za</sub>ti<sub>o</sub>n <sub>o</sub>n $\boldsymbol { Q }$

For example, Model-Agnostic Meta-Learning (MAML) [81] learns an initialization � that

<table><tr><td>Approach</td><td>Core Principle</td><td>Representative Methods</td><td>Strengths / Limitations</td></tr><tr><td>Metric-based</td><td>Learn a shared embedding space where classification is performed via similarity to class prototypes or exemplars. Often trained episodi- cally.</td><td>Prototypical Nets [80], Matching Nets [76], Siamese Nets [77], RelationNet [78]</td><td>+ Simple, fast inference; non- parametric adaptation. - Sensitive to embedding qual- ity; vulnerable to domain shift.</td></tr><tr><td>Optimization-based</td><td>Meta-learn an initialization or up- date rule that enables rapid adapta- tion to new tasks with few gradient steps.</td><td>MAML [81], Reptile [82], Meta-SGD [83], ANIL [84], tion. ProtoMAML [85]</td><td>+ Strong task-level generaliza- – Computationally heavy; un- stable in high dimensions.</td></tr><tr><td>Data Augmentation / Generative</td><td>Enrich support data via synthetic samples or feature-level perturba- tions using generative models or semantic mixing.</td><td>FeatGen [86], GAN- based [87], VAE-based [88], diversity. based [90]</td><td>+ Mitigates scarcity; improves Diffusion-based [89], CLIP- – Risk of unrealistic or biased samples.</td></tr><tr><td>Self-Supervised Contrastive</td><td>Learn transferable representations via contrastive or self-supervised pretraining, then adapt to few-shot tasks.</td><td>MoCo [91], SimCLR [92], BYOL [93], SwAV [95]</td><td>+ Strong embeddings; reduces DINO [94], label reliance. – Pretraining requires massive unlabeled data.</td></tr><tr><td>Transformer-based / Prompting</td><td>Use attention-based architectures or prompts to model task structure and leverage foundation models.</td><td>4V-assisted FSL Flamingo [43]</td><td>Few-shot Transformers [96], + Scales with LLMs; inter- Prompt-tuning [97], GPT- pretable task conditioning. [98], – Prompt sensitivity; high com- pute demand.</td></tr></table>

$$
\theta ^ { \prime } = \theta - \alpha \nabla _ { \theta } \mathcal { L } _ { S } ( f _ { \theta } ) ,

<sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> $\alpha$ i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>a</sub>d<sub>ap</sub>t<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>o</sub>n <sub>s</sub>t<sub>ep</sub> <sub>s</sub>i<sub>ze</sub> <sub>a</sub>nd $\mathcal { L } s$ i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k<sub>-spec</sub>ifi<sub>c</sub> l<sub>oss</sub> <sub>compu</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>on</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>suppor</sub>t <sub>se</sub>t �. The meta-objective is then to minimize the query loss after adaptation:

$$
\operatorname* { m i n } _ { \theta } \mathbb { E } _ { \mathcal { T } \sim p ( \mathcal { T } ) } \left[ \mathcal { L } _ { Q } ( f _ { \theta ^ { \prime } } ) \right] ,
$$

## 3.4 EXPERIMENTS AND RESULTS

## 3.4.1 DATASETS AND PERFORMANCE MEASURE

In this work, we em<sub>p</sub>lo<sub>y</sub>ed three <sub>p</sub>rimar<sub>y</sub> datasets: VidOR [113], Ima<sub>g</sub>eNet-VidVRD [114], and O<sub>p</sub>en Ima<sub>g</sub>es [115]. Extracted target frames from both datasets were paired <sub>w</sub>ith O<sub>pen</sub> I<sub>mages.</sub> S<sub>ome</sub> l<sub>ow-</sub>f<sub>requency</sub> <sub>c</sub>l<sub>asses</sub> <sub>were</sub> <sub>exc</sub>l<sub>u</sub>d<sub>e</sub>d d<sub>ur</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> t<sub>ra</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>ng,</sub> b<sub>u</sub>t <sub>a</sub>ll f<sub>rames</sub> <sub>were</sub> <sub>use</sub>d i<sub>n</sub> t<sub>es</sub>ti<sub>ng.</sub> N<sub>o</sub>t<sub>e</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>our</sub> <sub>me</sub>th<sub>o</sub>d i<sub>s</sub> <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ass-agnos</sub>ti<sub>c</sub> <sub>an</sub>d d<sub>oes</sub> <sub>no</sub>t <sub>use</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub> i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>orma</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> d<sub>ur</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> t<sub>ra</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>an</sub>d t<sub>es</sub>ti<sub>ng.</sub> D<sub>a</sub>t<sub>ase</sub>t <sub>s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>s</sub>ti<sub>cs</sub> <sub>are</sub> <sub>prov</sub>id<sub>e</sub>d i<sub>n</sub> T<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> 3<sub>.</sub>1<sub>.</sub>

## 3.6 SUMMARY

In this cha<sub>p</sub>ter, we introduced the novel class-a<sub>g</sub>nostic <sub>q</sub>uer<sub>y</sub>-<sub>g</sub>uided DETR for videos (QDETRv), e<sup>f</sup>ective<sup>l</sup>y en<sup>h</sup>ancing vi<sup>d</sup>eo o<sup>b</sup>ject <sup>l</sup>oca<sup>l</sup>ization using a query image an<sup>d</sup> tempora<sup>l</sup> context. We <sub>s</sub>h<sub>owe</sub>d <sub>eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>ti<sub>ons</sub> <sub>on</sub> t<sub>wo</sub> <sub>ex</sub>t<sub>en</sub>d<sub>e</sub>d d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>ase</sub>t<sub>s</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t di<sub>sp</sub>l<sub>aye</sub>d <sub>super</sub>i<sub>or</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>cons</sub>i<sub>s</sub>t<sub>en</sub>t <sub>per</sub>f<sub>ormance</sub> of QDETRv, <sub>p</sub>articularl<sub>y</sub> when <sub>p</sub>aired with video-s<sub>p</sub>ecific unsu<sub>p</sub>ervised <sub>p</sub>retrainin<sub>g</sub>. While <sub>s</sub>h<sub>ow</sub>in<sub>g</sub> <sub>p</sub>r<sub>o</sub>mi<sub>se,</sub> <sub>ou</sub>r <sub>app</sub>r<sub>oac</sub>h h<sub>as</sub> <sub>a</sub> f<sub>ew</sub> limit<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>o</sub>n<sub>s,</sub> in<sub>c</sub>l<sub>u</sub>din<sub>g</sub> limit<sub>e</sub>d <sub>success</sub> in l<sub>oca</sub>li<sub>z</sub>in<sub>g</sub> sma<sup>ll</sup> or occ<sup>l</sup>u<sup>d</sup>e<sup>d</sup> o<sup>b</sup>ject instances. ## 4.3 METHOD

Few-shot video object detection (FSVOD) aims to detect novel object categories that were not encountered during training. The task involves a support set containing a few images of novel object classes and a target video consisting of multiple frames. The goal is to identify and localize a<sup>ll</sup> instances o<sup>f</sup> t<sup>h</sup>e o<sup>b</sup>jects <sup>f</sup>rom t<sup>h</sup>e support set across every <sup>f</sup>rame o<sup>f</sup> t<sup>h</sup>e target vi<sup>d</sup>eo, as s<sup>h</sup>own i<sub>n</sub> Fi<sub>gure</sub> 4<sub>.</sub>1<sub>.</sub>

## FEW-SHOT DETECTION HEADS:

Th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>empora</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> <sub>en</sub>h<sub>ance</sub>d f<sub>rame</sub> <sub>em</sub>b<sub>e</sub>ddi<sub>ngs</sub> $\hat { \mathbf { F } } _ { t }$ <sub>are</sub> <sub>processe</sub>d th<sub>roug</sub>h <sub>para</sub>ll<sub>e</sub>l <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub>ifi<sub>ca</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>an</sub>d l<sub>oca</sub>li<sub>za</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> h<sub>ea</sub>d<sub>s</sub> t<sub>o</sub> <sub>pro</sub>d<sub>uce</sub> th<sub>e</sub> fi<sub>na</sub>l d<sub>e</sub>t<sub>ec</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>resu</sub>lt<sub>s.</sub>

It projects <sup>f</sup>rame em<sup>b</sup>e<sup>dd</sup>ings to t<sup>h</sup>e c<sup>l</sup>assi<sup>fi</sup>cation <sup>s</sup>p<sup>ace:</sup>

Non-temporal Methods. To establish strong non-temporal baselines, we compare against several ima<sub>g</sub>e-based few-shot detectors as discussed and com<sub>p</sub>ared in [110]. Traditional object detectors like Faster R-CNN [8] serve as fundamental baselines to validate the benefits of few-shot learnin<sub>g</sub> techni<sub>q</sub>ues. We include s<sub>p</sub>ecialized few-shot detectors from [110] such as FSOD [117], RDN [139]. Further we included CoAE [11] desi<sub>g</sub>ned for one-shot object detection and we extended it for f<sub>ew-s</sub>h<sub>o</sub>t d<sub>e</sub>t<sub>ec</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> b<sub>y</sub> fi<sub>n</sub>di<sub>ng</sub> th<sub>e</sub> b<sub>es</sub>t <sub>con</sub>fid<sub>en</sub>t d<sub>e</sub>t<sub>ec</sub>ti<sub>ons.</sub> C<sub>o</sub>AE l<sub>everage</sub> <sub>me</sub>t<sub>a-</sub>l<sub>earn</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>a</sub>tt<sub>en</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>mec</sub>h<sub>an</sub>i<sub>sms</sub> t<sub>o</sub> h<sub>an</sub>dl<sub>e</sub> li<sub>m</sub>it<sub>e</sub>d t<sub>ra</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>a.</sub> R<sub>ecen</sub>t t<sub>rans</sub>f<sub>ormer-</sub>b<sub>ase</sub>d <sub>approac</sub>h<sub>es</sub> like OWL-ViT [6] and UP-DETR [129] are included to evaluate the efectiveness of lar<sub>g</sub>e-scale <sub>p</sub>re-trainin<sub>g</sub>. We also com<sub>p</sub>are a<sub>g</sub>ainst a retrieval-based methods [140] that consider few-shot d<sub>e</sub>t<sub>ec</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>as</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>re</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>eva</sub>l f<sub>rom</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>poo</sub>l <sub>o</sub>f <sub>proposa</sub>l<sub>s.</sub>

Temporal Methods. For temporal modeling comparisons, we include several video-based a<sub>pp</sub>roaches. ## 4.4.13 ERROR TYPE ANALYSIS

Followin<sub>g</sub> TIDE [146], we anal<sub>y</sub>ze error t<sub>yp</sub>es in Fi<sub>g</sub>. 4.6. Classification errors dominate all a<sub>pp</sub>roaches (19-28 AP@0.5), with our method achievin<sub>g</sub> lowest classification (19) and localization (5) errors. Video-OWLViT excels in back<sub>g</sub>round handlin<sub>g</sub> (0.7) but sufers from hi<sub>g</sub>h classification errors (28), while QDETRv <sub>p</sub>erforms the <sub>p</sub>oorest overall. ## 5.4 EXPERIMENTS AND RESULTS

## 5.4.1 DATASETS

We have used two video benchmark datasets, namel<sub>y</sub>, VidOR [113, 151] and Ima<sub>g</sub>eNet-VidVRD [114] in our ex<sub>p</sub>eriments. ## 6.3 BACKGROUND: DYNAMIC TIME WARPING

D<sub>y</sub>namic Time War<sub>p</sub>in<sub>g</sub> (DTW) [208] is a widel<sub>y</sub> used method for ali<sub>g</sub>nin<sub>g</sub> and com<sub>p</sub>arin<sub>g</sub> t<sub>wo</sub> <sub>sequences,</sub> <sub>suc</sub>h <sub>as</sub> ti<sub>me</sub> <sub>ser</sub>i<sub>es,</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>may</sub> <sub>vary</sub> i<sub>n</sub> l<sub>eng</sub>th <sub>or</sub> <sub>ex</sub>hibit l<sub>oca</sub>l <sub>acce</sub>l<sub>era</sub>ti<sub>ons</sub> <sub>an</sub>d d<sub>ece</sub>l<sub>era</sub>ti<sub>ons.</sub> U<sub>n</sub>lik<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e</sub> E<sub>uc</sub>lid<sub>ean</sub> di<sub>s</sub>t<sub>ance,</sub> <sub>w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h <sub>requ</sub>i<sub>res</sub> <sub>sequences</sub> t<sub>o</sub> b<sub>e</sub> <sub>o</sub>f th<sub>e</sub> <sub>same</sub> l<sub>eng</sub>th <sub>an</sub>d <sub>a</sub>li<sub>gne</sub>d i<sub>n</sub> ti<sub>me,</sub> DTW i<sub>s</sub> <sub>ro</sub>b<sub>us</sub>t t<sub>o</sub> t<sub>empora</sub>l <sub>s</sub>hift<sub>s</sub> <sub>an</sub>d di<sub>s</sub>t<sub>or</sub>ti<sub>ons,</sub> <sub>ma</sub>ki<sub>ng</sub> it <sub>a</sub> <sub>popu</sub>l<sub>ar</sub> <sub>c</sub>h<sub>o</sub>i<sub>ce</sub> in s<sub>p</sub>eech, <sub>g</sub>esture, and time-series anal<sub>y</sub>sis [209].

Let $\mathbf { X } = [ \pmb { x } _ { 1 } , \dots , \pmb { x } _ { m } ] \in \mathbb { R } ^ { m \times d }$ <sub>an</sub>d $\mathbf { Y } = [ \mathbf { y } _ { 1 } , \dots , \mathbf { y } _ { n } ] \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n \times d }$ d<sub>eno</sub>t<sub>e</sub> t<sub>wo sequences o</sub>f <sub>poss</sub>ibl<sub>y</sub> dif<sub>eren</sub>t l<sub>eng</sub>th<sub>s</sub> <sub>�</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>�,</sub> <sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> � i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>ea</sub>t<sub>ure</sub> di<sub>mens</sub>i<sub>on.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> <sub>pa</sub>i<sub>rw</sub>i<sub>se</sub> di<sub>ss</sub>i<sub>m</sub>il<sub>ar</sub>it<sub>y</sub> b<sub>e</sub>t<sub>ween</sub> <sub>seque</sub>n<sub>ce</sub> <sub>e</sub>l<sub>e</sub>m<sub>e</sub>nt<sub>s</sub> i<sub>s</sub> <sub>e</sub>n<sub>co</sub>d<sub>e</sub>d in <sub>a</sub> <sub>cos</sub>t m<sub>a</sub>trix $\mathbf { C } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { m \times n }$ <sub>, w</sub>ith <sub>en</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>es</sub> $C _ { i , j } = \delta ( \mathbf x _ { i } , \mathbf y _ { j } )$ f<sub>or a c</sub>h<sub>osen</sub> distance function � (e.g., squared Euclidean).

An alignment between X and Y is represented by a binary matrix $\mathbf { A } \in \{ 0 , 1 \} ^ { m \times n }$ <sub>,</sub> <sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> $\mathbf { A } _ { i , j } = 1$ i<sub>n</sub>di<sub>ca</sub>t<sub>es</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t $\pmb { x } _ { i }$ i<sub>s</sub> <sub>ma</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d t<sub>o</sub> $\mathbf { y } _ { j }$ <sub>.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> <sub>se</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f <sub>a</sub>ll <sub>va</sub>lid <sub>a</sub>li<sub>gnmen</sub>t<sub>s,</sub> $\mathcal { A } _ { m , n } ,$ <sub>en</sub>f<sub>orces</sub> m<sub>o</sub>n<sub>o</sub>t<sub>o</sub>ni<sub>c</sub>it<sub>y</sub> <sub>a</sub>nd <sub>co</sub>ntin<sub>u</sub>it<sub>y</sub> <sub>co</sub>n<sub>s</sub>tr<sub>a</sub>int<sub>s</sub> <sub>so</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>a</sub>li<sub>g</sub>nm<sub>e</sub>nt<sub>s</sub> <sub>a</sub>r<sub>e</sub> <sub>co</sub>nti<sub>guous</sub> <sub>a</sub>nd <sub>o</sub>rd<sub>e</sub>r<sub>-p</sub>r<sub>ese</sub>r<sub>v</sub>in<sub>g.</sub>

DTW <sub>compu</sub>t<sub>es</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>m</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>mum</sub> t<sub>o</sub>t<sub>a</sub>l <sub>a</sub>li<sub>gnmen</sub>t <sub>cos</sub>t <sub>over</sub> <sub>a</sub>ll <sub>va</sub>lid <sub>a</sub>li<sub>gnmen</sub>t<sub>s:</sub>

$$
\operatorname { D T W } ( \mathbf { X } , \mathbf { Y } ) = \operatorname* { m i n } _ { \mathbf { A } \in \mathcal { A } _ { m , n } } \langle \mathbf { A } , \mathbf { C } \rangle ,
$$

<sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> $\begin{array} { r } { \langle \mathbf { A } , \mathbf { C } \rangle = \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { m } \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { n } \mathbf { A } _ { i , j } C _ { i , j } } \end{array}$ i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e</sub> F<sub>ro</sub>b<sub>en</sub>i<sub>us</sub> i<sub>nner</sub> <sub>pro</sub>d<sub>uc</sub>t<sub>.</sub> Thi<sub>s</sub> <sub>op</sub>ti<sub>m</sub>i<sub>za</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>can</sub> b<sub>e</sub> <sub>e</sub>fi<sub>c</sub>i<sub>e</sub>ntl<sub>y</sub> <sub>so</sub>l<sub>ve</sub>d b<sub>y</sub> d<sub>y</sub>n<sub>a</sub>mi<sub>c</sub> <sub>p</sub>r<sub>og</sub>r<sub>a</sub>mmin<sub>g</sub> in $O ( m n )$ tim<sub>e</sub> <sub>a</sub>nd <sub>space.</sub>

Limitations DTW’s flexibility enables robust sequence comparison and alignment, even when <sub>sequences</sub> <sub>are</sub> <sub>s</sub>t<sub>re</sub>t<sub>c</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d <sub>or</sub> <sub>compresse</sub>d i<sub>n</sub> ti<sub>me.</sub> H<sub>owever,</sub> <sub>a</sub> k<sub>ey</sub> li<sub>m</sub>it<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>o</sub>f DTW i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t it i<sub>s</sub> n<sub>o</sub>t dif<sub>e</sub>r<sub>e</sub>nti<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> <sub>w</sub>ith r<sub>espec</sub>t t<sub>o</sub> it<sub>s</sub> in<sub>pu</sub>t<sub>s,</sub> <sub>as</sub> th<sub>e</sub> minim<sub>u</sub>m <sub>ove</sub>r <sub>a</sub>li<sub>g</sub>nm<sub>e</sub>nt<sub>s</sub> intr<sub>o</sub>d<sub>uces</sub> di<sub>scon</sub>ti<sub>nu</sub>iti<sub>es.</sub> Thi<sub>s</sub> <sub>non-</sub>dif<sub>eren</sub>ti<sub>a</sub>bilit<sub>y</sub> <sub>ma</sub>k<sub>es</sub> DTW <sub>unsu</sub>it<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> <sub>as</sub> <sub>a</sub> l<sub>oss</sub> f<sub>unc</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> i<sub>n</sub> <sub>gra</sub>di<sub>en</sub>t<sub>-</sub> b<sub>ase</sub>d l<sub>earn</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> f<sub>ramewor</sub>k<sub>s.</sub>

Soft-DTW Diferentiable Relaxation To address this, soft-DTW [209] replaces the hard minimum in DTW with a diferentiable soft-minimum (lo<sub>g</sub>-sum-ex<sub>p</sub>) o<sub>p</sub>erator, <sub>p</sub>arameterized <sup>b</sup><sub>y</sub> a smoot<sup>hi</sup>n<sub>g</sub> <sub>p</sub>arameter $\gamma > 0$ <sub>.</sub> Th<sub>e so</sub>ft<sub>-m</sub>i<sub>n</sub>i<sub>mum opera</sub>t<sub>or</sub> i<sub>s</sub> d<sub>e</sub>fi<sub>ne</sub>d <sub>as:</sub>

$$
\operatorname* { m i n } ^ { \gamma } \{ a _ { 1 } , \dots , a _ { K } \} = - \gamma \log \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { K } \exp \left( - \frac { a _ { k } } { \gamma } \right) .
$$

Th<sub>e</sub> <sub>so</sub>ft<sub>-</sub>DTW <sub>cos</sub>t i<sub>s</sub> th<sub>en</sub> <sub>g</sub>i<sub>ven</sub> b<sub>y:</sub>

$$

$$

Overall Loss. The overall loss function $\mathcal { L }$ is compute<sup>d</sup> as a com<sup>b</sup>ination o<sup>f</sup> mu<sup>l</sup>tip<sup>l</sup>e o<sup>b</sup>jectives, accountin<sub>g</sub> for ali<sub>g</sub>nment costs (<sub>p</sub>re- and <sub>p</sub>ost-fusion ali<sub>g</sub>nment), fore<sub>g</sub>round/back<sub>g</sub>round c<sup>l</sup>assi<sup>fi</sup>cation, an<sup>d</sup> <sup>b</sup>oun<sup>d</sup>ary <sup>l</sup>oca<sup>l</sup>ization. For � training samp<sup>l</sup>es, t<sup>h</sup>is mu<sup>l</sup>ti-o<sup>b</sup>jective overa<sup>ll</sup> <sup>l</sup>oss i<sub>s</sub> d<sub>e</sub>fi<sub>ne</sub>d <sub>as:</sub>

$$
\mathcal { L } = \frac { 1 } { S } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { S } \left( \lambda _ { f g } \mathcal { L } _ { f g } + \lambda _ { \mathrm { s e g } } \mathcal { L } _ { \mathrm { s e g } } + \lambda _ { \mathrm { a l i g n } } ^ { \mathrm { p r e } } \mathcal { L } _ { \mathrm { a l i g n } } ^ { \mathrm { p r e } } + \lambda _ { \mathrm { a l i g n } } ^ { \mathrm { p o s t } } \mathcal { L } _ { \mathrm { a l i g n } } ^ { \mathrm { p o s t } } \right) ,
$$

where the �s control the importance of each component.

## 6.6.1 BASELINES

## FULLY-SUPERVISED VID2VIDMR METHODS

We compare with Vid2VidMR methods like CGBM [173], GDP [23], SRL [25], FFI+SRM [22], SST [216] and Video-level match [173].
